We formulate LoRA compression as a reconstruction problem, where the goal is to approximate the original adapters via collections of matrices of a smaller size.\\\\n\\\\nAs a result, work that builds on LoRA enjoys a practical advantage due to its broad acceptance in practice. There are many efforts to compress models (Cheng et al., 2017; Gholami et al., 2022; Sharma et al., 2024; Li et al., 2018). Predominantly, pruning and sparsification methods delete weights (Yadav et al., 2023a), and quantization methods reduce the weights\\\\u2019 precision (Dettmers et al., 2024). Some works compress weights to reduce model size but typically require decompression and hence do not save GPU memory (Hershcovitch et al., 2024). Similarly to our work, a few note increased performance and generalization after compression (Yadav et al., 2023a; Nadjahi et al., 2023; Hershcovitch et al., 2024; Sharma et al., 2024). Our work also relates to model merging (Choshen et al., 2022; Wortsman et al., 2022; Matena & Raffel, 2021) and mixtures of experts (Muqeeth et al., 2024; Yadav et al., 2024).\\\\n\\\\nLoRA\\\\u2019s popularity has triggered a growing need to serve large collections of LoRA adapters at scale. Proprietary and open-source LLM providers offer fine-tuning services (OpenAI, 2024; TogetherAI, 2024; Predibase, 2024) with user bases likely in the thousands or even hundreds of thousands. As each user wants to use their own fine-tuned version of the LLM, serving a dedicated fine-tuned LLM per user becomes infeasible. To this end, S-LoRA (Sheng et al., 2023) proposes a system where only the base LLM is placed on an inference server and individual LoRA adapters are switched as needed at inference time. S-LoRA optimizes the system\\\\u2019s inner workings via custom CUDA kernels and memory management to increase throughput when serving multiple LoRAs. Multi-LoRA system design has also been adopted in vLLM (Kwon et al., 2023), a state-of-the-art LLM serving engine.\\\\n\\\\nThe problem of accommodating multiple LoRA adapters is also apparent when placing LLMs on edge devices, where smaller LLMs are fine-tuned for various tasks, and the adapters are swapped depending on the task at hand (Gunter et al., 2024). In this setting, the number of adapters is smaller, e.g., a few dozen (Gunter et al., 2024), but the memory constraints are also more stringent. In this work, we consider the problem of compressing a collection of LoRAs. We have two key objectives: (1) preserving the performance of the original LoRAs and (2) improving the throughput of serving many LoRAs. We formulate LoRA compression as a reconstruction problem, where the goal is to approximate the original adapters via collections of matrices of a smaller size. We compress LoRAs jointly by finding a shared basis and LoRA-specific scaling matrices and propose a joint diagonalization-based algorithm (JD).\\\\n\\\\nSeveral works improve LoRA (Liu et al., 2024; Wang et al., 2024), sometimes with algebraic methods like SVD (Meng et al., 2024; Zhang et al., 2023; Jiang et al., 2023b) or by leveraging its statistical properties (Zhu et al., 2024; Zeng & Lee, 2024). Relatively few, however, accelerate inference times. S-LoRA (Sheng et al., 2023) provides an efficient means of switching between LoRAs. Wen & Chaudhuri (2024) adapt training to reduce batch multiplications, accelerating inference. Our method achieves a similar outcome (see Appendix D) without changing the LoRA formulation or requiring that LoRAs be trained in a dedicated way; future improvements to LoRA will also benefit from this aspect of our work (e.g., Meng et al. (2024)). Punica (Chen et al., 2023) introduces Segmented Gather Matrix-Vector Multiplication (SGMV) to optimize multi-LoRA serving by parallelizing feature-weight multiplications in batches and grouping requests that use the same LoRA.\\\",\\n        \\\"is_citable\\\": true,\\n        \\\"prompt_context_metadata\\\": {\\n          \\\"age\\\": \\\"May 29, 2025\\\",\\n          \\\"url\\\": \\\"https://arxiv.org/html/2407.00066v4\\\",\\n          \\\"search_provider\\\": \\\"anthropic\\\"\\n        }\\n      },\\n      {\\n        \\\"type\\\": \\\"knowledge\\\",\\n        \\\"title\\\": \\\"GitHub - S-LoRA/S-LoRA: S-LoRA: Serving Thousands of Concurrent LoRA Adapters\\\",\\n        \\\"url\\\": \\\"https://github.com/S-LoRA/S-LoRA\\\",\\n        \\\"metadata\\\": {\\n          \\\"type\\\": \\\"webpage_metadata\\\",\\n          \\\"site_domain\\\": \\\"github.com\\\",\\n          \\\"favicon_url\\\": \\\"https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?sz=64&domain=github.com\\\",\\n          \\\"site_name\\\": \\\"GitHub\\\"\\n        },\\n        \\\"is_missing\\\": false,\\n        \\\"text\\\": \\\"Unified Paging uses a unified memory pool to manage dynamic adapter weights with different ranks and KV cache tensors with varying sequence lengths. Additionally, S-LoRA employs a novel tensor parallelism strategy and highly optimized custom CUDA kernels for heterogeneous batching of LoRA computation. Collectively, these features enable S-LoRA to serve thousands of LoRA adapters on a single GPU or across multiple GPUs with a small overhead. Compared to state-of-the-art libraries such as HuggingFace PEFT and vLLM (with naive support of LoRA serving), S-LoRA can improve the throughput by up to 4 times and increase the number of served adapters by several orders of magnitude. As a result, S-LoRA enables scalable serving of many task-specific fine-tuned models and offers the potential for large-scale customized fine-tuning services. ... Recommended: GPUs from the Ampere family, like the A100, which support bfloat16 operations.\\\\n\\\\nA fair scheduler VTC (paper) has been integrated into S-LoRA. See file slora/server/router/vtc_req_queue.py. The \\\\\\\"pretrain-then-finetune\\\\\\\" paradigm is commonly adopted in the deployment of large language models. Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method, is often employed to adapt a base model to a multitude of tasks, resulting in a substantial collection of LoRA adapters derived from one base model. We observe that this paradigm presents significant opportunities for batched inference during serving. To capitalize on these opportunities, we present S-LoRA, a system designed for the scalable serving of many LoRA adapters. S-LoRA stores all adapters in the main memory and fetches the adapters used by the currently running queries to the GPU memory. To efficiently use the GPU memory and reduce fragmentation, S-LoRA proposes Unified Paging.\\\\n\\\\nUnified Paging uses a unified memory pool to manage dynamic adapter weights with different ranks and KV cache tensors with varying sequence lengths. Additionally, S-LoRA employs a novel tensor parallelism strategy and highly optimized custom CUDA kernels for heterogeneous batching of LoRA computation. Collectively, these features enable S-LoRA to serve thousands of LoRA adapters on a single GPU or across multiple GPUs with a small overhead. Compared to state-of-the-art libraries such as HuggingFace PEFT and vLLM (with naive support of LoRA serving), S-LoRA can improve the throughput by up to 4 times and increase the number of served adapters by several orders of magnitude. As a result, S-LoRA enables scalable serving of many task-specific fine-tuned models and offers the potential for large-scale customized fine-tuning services. ... Recommended: GPUs from the Ampere family, like the A100, which support bfloat16 operations.\\\\n\\\\nUnified Paging: To reduce memory fragmentation and increase batch size, S-LoRA introduces a unified memory pool. This pool manages dynamic adapter weights and KV cache tensors by a unified paging mechanism. Heterogeneous Batching: To minimize the latency overhead when batching different adapters of varying ranks, S-LoRA employs highly optimized custom CUDA kernels. These kernels operate directly on non-contiguous memory and align with the memory pool design, facilitating efficient batched inference for added LoRA computation. S-LoRA TP: To ensure effective parallelization across multiple GPUs, S-LoRA introduces a novel tensor parallelism strategy. This approach incurs minimal communication cost for the added LoRA computation compared to that of the base model. This is realized by scheduling communications on small intermediate tensors and fusing them with the communications of the base model.\\\",\\n        \\\"is_citable\\\": true,\\n    \\\"site_name\\\": \\\"arXiv\\\"\\n        },\\n        \\\"is_missing\\\": false,\\n        \\\"text\\\": \\\"Another way we can examine differences between LoRA and full fine-tuning is to calculate the effective rank (Roy &amp; Vetterli, 2007) of the change made to the weights during fine-tuning. As shown in Fig. 6, when we calculate this we observe that the effective rank of full fine-tuning solutions have a significantly higher effective rank than solutions learned by LoRA, even when LoRA has high rank. Even at high adapter ranks and with rank stabilization, we find across layers that the effective rank of LoRA updates is less than half that of full fine-tuning and a quarter of the adapter rank. For example, with the high rank of ... italic_r = 768 for RoBERTa, LoRA updates have an average effective rank of 300. This suggests that LoRA is under utilizing its full capacity ... italic_r, and may help explain observed gaps between LoRA and full fine-tuning on challenging tasks like coding (Biderman et al., 2024; Zhuo et al., 2024).\\\\n\\\\nAnother way we can examine differences between LoRA and full fine-tuning is to calculate the effective rank (Roy & Vetterli, 2007) of the change made to the weights during fine-tuning. As shown in Fig. 6, when we calculate this we observe that the effective rank of full fine-tuning solutions have a significantly higher effective rank than solutions learned by LoRA, even when LoRA has high rank. Even at high adapter ranks and with rank stabilization, we find across layers that the effective rank of LoRA updates is less than half that of full fine-tuning and a quarter of the adapter rank. For example, with the high rank of ... italic_r = 768 for RoBERTa, LoRA updates have an average effective rank of 300. This suggests that LoRA is under utilizing its full capacity ... italic_r, and may help explain observed gaps between LoRA and full fine-tuning on challenging tasks like coding (Biderman et al., 2024; Zhuo et al., 2024).\\\\n\\\\nPre-trained language models offer a foundation for downstream applications, eliminating the need to train from scratch (Ouyang et al., 2022; Devlin et al., 2019). Full fine-tuning, in which every parameter of a pre-trained model is updated, has been used for adaptation (Devlin et al., 2019; Liu et al., 2019). Low Rank Adaptation (LoRA; Hu et al., 2021), which represents the update to the weights as a product of two low-rank matrices, reduces computation and memory requirements relative to full fine-tuning. Past work has shown that LoRA matches full fine-tuning performance for tasks like sequence classification (Hu et al., 2021), instruction tuning (Dettmers et al., 2023; Ghosh et al., 2024), and chat (Dettmers et al., 2023). Other work has shown a gap in the performance of full fine-tuning and LoRA on harder tasks like code generation (Biderman et al., 2024; Zhuo et al., 2024).\\\\n\\\\nRecent work comparing LoRA to full fine-tuning has found that LoRA forgets less on previously learned information (Biderman et al., 2024) and more closely resembles the pre-trained model (Ghosh et al., 2024). Surprisingly, some experiments in the current study show opposite trends. However, there are significant differences in the datasets used for evaluation\\\\u2014(Biderman et al., 2024) investigated instruction tuning for language generation, while we mainly study sequence labeling tasks. Importantly, Biderman et al. (2024) study conditions when LoRA fine-tuned models fail to fit the adaptation task as well as full-finetuned models, and as a result also forget less of the pre-training distribution. However, we study models where the LoRA achieves the same performance as full fine-tuning, comparing generalization behavior at a fixed target task accuracy.\\\\n\\\\nWhile this parameterization is empirically effective, a principled explanation of the mechanism by which it matches the full fine-tuning performance has remained elusive. One explanation is offered by the intrinsic dimension hypothesis (Li et al., 2018; Aghajanyan et al., 2021), which posits that the update learned via fine-tuning has an intrinsically low intrinsic rank, suggesting that LoRA might recover an approximately equivalent solution to full fine-tuning. However, prior work has observed differences in the ability of LoRA and full fine-tuning to independently change the angle and magnitude with which a neuron transforms its input (Liu et al., 2024). Moreover, other work has also observed that LoRA has difficulty matching the performance of full fine-tuning on harder tasks, like code generation (Biderman et al., 2024; Zhuo et al., 2024) and long-form text generation (Ivison et al., 2023).\\\",\\n        \\\"is_citable\\\": true,\\n        \\\"prompt_context_metadata\\\": {\\n          \\\"age\\\": \\\"October 28, 2024\\\",\\n          \\\"url\\\": \\\"https://arxiv.org/html/2410.21228v1\\\",\\n          \\\"search_provider\\\": \\\"anthropic\\\"\\n        }\\n      },\\n      {\\n        \\\"type\\\": \\\"knowledge\\\",\\n        \\\"title\\\": \\\"5 Reasons Why LoRA Adapters are the Future of Fine-tuning\\\",\\n        \\\"url\\\": \\\"https://predibase.com/blog/5-reasons-why-lora-adapters-are-the-future-of-fine-tuning\\\",\\n        \\\"metadata\\\": {\\n          \\\"type\\\": \\\"webpage_metadata\\\",\\n          \\\"site_domain\\\": \\\"predibase.com\\\",\\n          \\\"favicon_url\\\": \\\"https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?sz=64&domain=predibase.com\\\",\\n          \\\"site_name\\\": \\\"Predibase\\\"\\n        },\\n        \\\"is_missing\\\": false,\\n        \\\"text\\\": \\\"Adapters can also reduce the number of parameters that need to be updated, reducing the time and compute required\\\\u2014training overhead can be reduced up to 70% compared to full fine-tuning.\\\\n\\\\nThat's where adapters come in. Adapters allow the LLM to adapt to new scenarios without changing its original parameters. This preserves the LLM's general knowledge and avoids catastrophic forgetting when learning new tasks. Adapters can also reduce the number of parameters that need to be updated, reducing the time and compute required\\\\u2014training overhead can be reduced up to 70% compared to full fine-tuning. A popular and efficient technique for training adapters is \\\\u00b7 Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA). LoRA operates on the premise that the model is already near the optimal outcome and minimizes the need for extensive modifications. LoRA operates by freezing the original weights and only changing the difference in the cost function. To further compress, LoRA transforms the adapter into an optimized decomposition and subsequently lowers the rank of these matrices.\\\\n\\\\nNow that we have a baseline knowledge of fine-tuning, adapters and LoRA let\\\\u2019s dig into the reasons why adapters are the future of fine-tuning. ... a study published in 2021, LoRA was evaluated against complete fine-tuning of RoBERTa and DeBERTa across various benchmarks. The results indicated comparable performance levels overall, with slight superiority of full fine-tuning observed in tasks such as SST-2 (sentiment analysis). Conversely, in tasks like MNLI and on average, LoRA demonstrated superior performance compared to full fine-tuning specifically on DeBERTa. From LoRA paper: LoRA fine-tuned model performance on par with full fine-tuned model performance. If we look at the total amount of trainable parameters for each model below, you can see that RoBERTa large, full fine-tuned, is training in excess of 350 million parameters while DeBERTa XXL is training in excess of 1.5 billion parameters.\\\\n\\\\nTraditional fine-tuning updates all model parameters, while LoRA adapters modify only specific layers, reducing computational load and preserving the model's original knowledge base. LoRA fine-tuning freezes the original model weights and introduces additional trainable parameters in a low-rank decomposition, making the process more efficient. LoRA adapters maintain or even enhance model performance while significantly lowering the resource requirements compared to full fine-tuning. Implementing LoRA adapters in models like Llama-70B involves integrating adapter modules into the model architecture, allowing for efficient fine-tuning. LoRA reduces computational requirements and memory usage, enabling faster and more cost-effective fine-tuning of large models without compromising performance.\\\\n\\\\nWe recently hosted an episode of our ML Real Talk Series on the topic of adapter-based training and why techniques like LoRA (Low-rank Adaptation) are a game-changer for fine-tuning. Adapters provide a cost-effective and streamlined approach to leveraging open-source LLMs to achieve better performance at lower cost than other methods. In this blog, we cover the key takeaways from the conversation to help you get started using adapters for fine-tuning LLMs in your day-to-day work. It\\\\u2019s important to first understand the fundamentals so let\\\\u2019s start with key definitions: Fine-tuning: Fine-tuning plays a vital role in machine learning, particularly when dealing with extensive pre-trained open-source models like Mistral, Zephyr, and Gemma. The process of fine-tuning involves updating the model's parameters by feeding it new task-specific data and adjusting its weights based on the expected output.\\\",\\n        \\\"is_citable\\\": true,\\n        \\\"prompt_context_metadata\\\": {\\n          \\\"age\\\": \\\"June 10, 2024\\\",\\n          \\\"url\\\": \\\"https://predibase.com/blog/5-reasons-why-lora-adapters-are-the-future-of-fine-tuning\\\",\\n          \\\"search_provider\\\": \\\"anthropic\\\"\\n        }\\n      },\\n      {\\n        \\\"type\\\": \\\"knowledge\\\",\\n        \\\"title\\\": \\\"Efficient Fine-Tuning with LoRA: A Guide to Optimal Parameter Selection for Large Language Models\\\",\\n        \\\"url\\\": \\\"https://www.databricks.com/blog/efficient-fine-tuning-lora-guide-llms\\\",\\n        \\\"metadata\\\": {\\n          \\\"type\\\": \\\"webpage_metadata\\\",\\n          \\\"site_domain\\\": \\\"databricks.com\\\",\\n          \\\"favicon_url\\\": \\\"https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?sz=64&domain=databricks.com\\\",\\n          \\\"site_name\\\": \\\"Databricks\\\"\\n        },\\n        \\\"is_missing\\\": false,\\n        \\\"text\\\": \\\"Fortunately, there exist parameter-efficient approaches for fine-tuning that have proven to be effective. Although most such approaches have yielded less performance, Low Rank Adaptation (LoRA) has bucked this trend by even outperforming full finetuning in some cases, as a consequence of avoiding catastrophic forgetting (a phenomenon which occurs when the knowledge of the pretrained model is lost during the fine-tuning process). LoRA is an improved finetuning method where instead of finetuning all the weights that constitute the weight matrix of the pre-trained large language model, two smaller matrices that approximate this larger matrix are fine-tuned. These matrices constitute the LoRA adapter. This fine-tuned adapter is then loaded to the pretrained model and used for inference. QLoRA is an even more memory efficient version of LoRA where the pretrained model is loaded to GPU memory as quantized 4-bit weights (compared to 8-bits in the case of LoRA), while preserving similar effect\\\\n\\\\nFortunately, there exist parameter-efficient approaches for fine-tuning that have proven to be effective. Although most such approaches have yielded less performance, Low Rank Adaptation (LoRA) has bucked this trend by even outperforming full finetuning in some cases, as a consequence of avoiding catastrophic forgetting (a phenomenon which occurs when the knowledge of the pretrained model is lost during the fine-tuning process). LoRA is an improved finetuning method where instead of finetuning all the weights that constitute the weight matrix of the pre-trained large language model, two smaller matrices that approximate this larger matrix are fine-tuned. These matrices constitute the LoRA adapter. This fine-tuned adapter is then loaded to the pretrained model and used for inference. QLoRA is an even more memory efficient version of LoRA where the pretrained model is loaded to GPU memory as quantized 4-bit weights (compared to 8-bits in the case of LoRA), while preserving similar effect\\\\n\\\\nEnsuring efficient resource utilization and cost-effectiveness is crucial when choosing a strategy for fine-tuning. This blog explores arguably the most popular and effective variant of such parameter efficient methods, Low Rank Adaptation (LoRA), with a particular emphasis on QLoRA (an even more efficient variant of LoRA). The approach here will be to take an open large language model and fine-tune it to generate fictitious product descriptions when prompted with a product name and a category. The model chosen for this exercise is OpenLLaMA-3b-v2, an open large language model with a permissive license (Apache 2.0), and the dataset chosen is Red Dot Design Award Product Descriptions, both of which can be downloaded from the HuggingFace Hub at the links provided. In the realm of language models, fine tuning an existing language model to perform a specific task on specific data is a common practice.\\\\n\\\\nWhen using PEFT to train a model with LoRA or QLoRA (note that, as mentioned before, the primary difference between the two is that in the latter, the pretrained models are frozen in 4-bit during the fine-tuning process), the hyperparameters of the low rank adaptation process can be defined in a LoRA config as shown below: Two of these hyperparameters, r and target_modules are empirically shown to affect adaptation quality significantly and will be the focus of the tests that follow. The other hyperparameters are kept constant at the values indicated above for simplicity. r represents the rank of the low rank matrices learned during the finetuning process. As this value is increased, the number of parameters needed to be updated during the low-rank adaptation increases. Intuitively, a lower r may lead to a quicker, less computationally intensive training process, but may affect the quality of the model thus produced.\\\\n\\\\nHowever, increasing r beyond a certain value may not yield any discernible increase in quality of model output. How the value of r affects adaptation (fine-tuning) quality will be put to the test shortly. When fine-tuning with LoRA, it is possible to target specific modules in the model architecture. The adaptation process will target these modules and apply the update matrices to them. Similar to the situation with \\\\\\\"r,\\\\\\\" targeting more modules during LoRA adaptation results in increased training time and greater demand for compute resources. Thus, it is a common practice to only target the attention blocks of the transformer. However, recent work as shown in the QLoRA paper by Dettmers et al. suggests that targeting all linear layers results in better adaptation quality. This will be explored here as well. Now, on the dataset side: Say the fine-tuning dataset has 10,000 examples, each with 1,000 tokens \\\\u2192 10 million tokens total. Roughly speaking, if the LoRA adapter has 1.6M parameters, we\\\\u2019re trying to learn 1.6M degrees of freedom from 10M observations (tokens). That\\\\u2019s a data-to-parameter ratio of ~6:1. Empirically, the sweet spot is when this ratio is closer to 1.5:1 to 1.1:1. That\\\\u2019s when LoRA really starts matching full fine-tuning performance. Condition 2 (LoRA rank is higher) This one\\\\u2019s straightforward, increasing rank increases the number of trainable parameters, which pushes LoRA\\\\u2019s performance closer to full fine-tuning levels.\\\\n\\\\nFor RLHF, Low Ranks perform just as well, because LoRA = FullFT even with tiny rank (like 1). Why? RL contains very little information per episode, so even a rank-1 adapter has enough capacity. ... Supervised data gives ~O(tokens) bits. RL gives ~O(1) bit per episode. However, the unique characteristics of LoRA present key challenges for parallel fine-tuning LoRA adapters. Conceivably, the frozen base LLM in LoRA facilitates the parallel training of multiple LoRA adapters by sharing the same base model, which reduces the GPU memory footprint (i.e., requiring only one copy of the LLM) and enhances training parallelism (i.e., allowing simultaneous LoRA training tasks). Nevertheless, when fine-tuning massive LoRA adapters exceeds the capacity of a single GPU, multiple GPUs become necess\\\\n\\\\nMeanwhile, concurrently fine-tuning multiple adapters has become increasingly crucial: LLM platforms (llm, 2024b, c, a) enable developers to fine-tune multiple models and develop various domain-specific applications at the same time; for individual developers, selecting multiple sets of hyperparameters (e.g., learning rate or LoRA rank) either manually or automatically (Tribes et al., 2023) by fine-tuning multiple adapters can quickly reveal the best-performing adapter. However, the unique characteristics of LoRA present key challenges for parallel fine-tuning LoRA adapters. Conceivably, the frozen base LLM in LoRA facilitates the parallel training of multiple LoRA adapters by sharing the same base model, which reduces the GPU memory footprint (i.e., requiring only one copy of the LLM) and enhances training parallelism (i.e., allowing simultaneous LoRA training tasks). Nevertheless, when fine-tuning massive LoRA adapters exceeds the capacity of a single GPU, multiple GPUs become necess\\\\n\\\\nCUDA Graph (cud, 2019) addresses kernel launch overhead by providing a mechanism at the CUDA driver level that allows launching multiple GPU operations with a single CPU operation. Meanwhile, deep learning compilers (Ansel et al., 2024; Chen et al., 2018a) mitigate kernel launch overhead at the computational graph level through operator fusion. However, these methods do not support dynamic shapes, and the text data used in fine-tuning often varies in length. mLoRA tackles this issue by consolidating data from multiple fine-tuning tasks into one, reducing the number of operator calls and thus the kernel launch overhead. We have presented mLoRA, a fine-tuning system designed and developed for efficiently training multiple LoRA adapters across GPUs and machines. The proposed techniques, including LoRA-aware streamlined pipeline parallelism and a LoRA-efficient training operator, allow mLoRA to fully utilize the computational and memory capacities of a multiple-GPU training cluster.\\\\n\\\\nInstead, Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) methods (Han et al., 2024), including partial (Ding et al., 2023b; Zhao et al., 2020; Ansell et al., 2021), additive (Rebuffi et al., 2017; Houlsby et al., 2019; Lester et al., 2021a; Asai et al., 2022), and reparameterized (Hu et al., 2022) fine-tunings, have been developed. They train a much smaller set of parameters, thus cutting training costs while maintaining performance levels comparable to full-weight fine-tuning. Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) (Hu et al., 2022; Dettmers et al., 2024; Chavan et al., 2023), a popular class of PEFT methods, freezes the parameters of an LLM while updating pairs of low-rank matrices with far fewer parameters, namely adapter weights. Models fine-tuned with LoRA not only match but also exceed the performance of fully fine-tuned models while remaining extremely lightweight, e.g., requiring less than 1% of trainable parameters (Han et al., 2024; Zhao et al., 2024).\\\\n\\\\nFigure 1. Sharing pre-trained model weights for fine-tuning multiple LoRA adapters with reduced overhead. Training an LLM from scratch demands extensive computational resources over days of time, often utilizing thousands of GPUs and incurring significant financial costs (Shen et al., 2024; Bang et al., 2023). In contrast, fine-tuning pre-trained language models (PLMs) has made LLM benefits more accessible. Organizations like Meta and Google provide their PLMs, such as LLaMA (Touvron et al., 2023a) and Gemma (Team et al., 2024), to the public. Fine-tuning these models for various downstream tasks is effective (Patil and Gudivada, 2024) and offers a more cost-efficient way to harness LLM capabilities. Conventionally, full-weight fine-tuning of large-scale pre-trained models requires updating all parameters, which often incurs prohibitive computational costs.\\\",\\n        \\\"is_citable\\\": true,\\n        \\\"prompt_context_metadata\\\": {\\n          \\\"age\\\": \\\"September 18, 2024\\\",\\n          \\\"url\\\": \\\"https://arxiv.org/html/2312.02515v2\\\",\\n          \\\"search_provider\\\": \\\"anthropic\\\"\\n        }\\n      },\\n      {\\n        \\\"type\\\": \\\"knowledge\\\",\\n        \\\"title\\\": \\\"Gen AI Fine-Tuning Techniques: LoRA, QLoRA, and Adapters Compared\\\",\\n        \\\"url\\\": \\\"https://www.digitaldividedata.com/blog/ai-fine-tuning-techniques-lora-qlora-and-adapters\\\",\\n        \\\"metadata\\\": {\\n          \\\"type\\\": \\\"webpage_metadata\\\",\\n          \\\"site_domain\\\": \\\"digitaldividedata.com\\\",\\n          \\\"favicon_url\\\": \\\"https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?sz=64&domain=digitaldividedata.com\\\",\\n          \\\"site_name\\\": \\\"Digital Divide Data\\\"\\n        },\\n        \\\"is_missing\\\": false,\\n        \\\"text\\\": \\\"Quantized LoRA (QLoRA): Builds on LoRA by adding 4-bit quantization of model weights, enabling memory-efficient fine-tuning of very large models on consumer-grade GPUs. Adapters: Modular components inserted between transformer layers. These are small, trainable networks that adapt the behavior of the base model while keeping its original parameters frozen. The PEFT paradigm is especially useful in enterprise AI applications, where models need to be fine-tuned repeatedly across domains, such as legal, healthcare, or customer support, without incurring the cost of full retraining. It also aligns well with the growing trend of edge deployment, where smaller models with limited compute capacity still need high performance on specialized tasks. LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation), introduced by Microsoft Research in 2021, was one of the first techniques to demonstrate that large language models can be fine-tuned effectively by updating only a small number of parameters.\\\\n\\\\nFine-tuning Gen AI remains resource-intensive, often requiring access to high-end hardware, long training cycles, and substantial financial investment. In response to these limitations, a new class of fine-tuning strategies has emerged under tparameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT). Among these, three techniques have gained widespread attention: LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation), QLoRA (Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation), and Adapter-based fine-tuning. This blog takes a deep dive into three Gen AI fine-tuning techniques: LoRA, QLoRA, and Adapters, comparing their architectures, implementation complexity, hardware efficiency, and real-world applicability. Fine-tuning large language models has traditionally followed a full-parameter update approach, where all weights in a pretrained model are modified to adapt the model to a new downstream task. While effective in terms of task-specific performance, this method is computationally expensive, memory-intensive, and often infeasible for organizations wi\\\\n\\\\nIn light of these constraints, researchers and practitioners have shifted focus toward more efficient methods that minimize the number of updated parameters and memory footprint while retaining or even improving the performance of traditional fine-tuning. This is the context in which parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods such as LoRA, QLoRA, and Adapters have gained prominence. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) represents a strategic shift in how we adapt large language models to new tasks. Rather than updating all of a model\\\\u2019s parameters, PEFT methods selectively modify a small portion of the model or add lightweight, trainable components. This drastically reduces computational requirements, memory consumption, and storage overhead, all without significantly compromising performance. At its core, PEFT is based on the principle that the knowledge encoded in a pretrained LLM is broadly generalizable.\\\\n\\\\nContinual Learning: Adapters excel in multi-task and continual learning settings, as they isolate task-specific knowledge, reducing interference and catastrophic forgetting. ... Adapter-based fine-tuning is widely adopted in multilingual and multi-domain NLP settings. For instance, a single model serving across industries, legal, medical, and customer support, can load different adapters for each use case without modifying its core architecture. Some enterprise-scale implementations also combine adapters with LoRA or quantized models to balance inference efficiency and training flexibility. ... Adapters slightly increase inference time and model complexity due to the additional layers. Their effectiveness also varies with model architecture and task type, while highly effective for classification and NLU tasks, their gains in generative settings (e.g., summarization or dialogue) can sometimes be more modest compared to LoRA or QLoRA.\\\\n\\\\nQuantized LoRA (QLoRA): Builds on LoRA by adding 4-bit quantization of model weights, enabling memory-efficient fine-tuning of very large models on consumer-grade GPUs. Adapters: Modular components inserted between transformer layers. These are small, trainable networks that adapt the behavior of the base model while keeping its original parameters frozen. The PEFT paradigm is especially useful in enterprise AI applications, where models need to be fine-tuned repeatedly across domains, such as legal, healthcare, or customer support, without incurring the cost of full retraining. It also aligns well with the growing trend of edge deployment, where smaller models with limited compute capacity still need high performance on specialized tasks. Fine-tuning is the process of adjusting a pre-trained model to improve its performance on a particular task. This method significantly reduces the amount of time and computational resources required compared to training a new model from scratch. Fine-tuning allows models to leverage learned features that are applicable to the new task, enhancing efficiency and adaptability. Traditional fine-tuning methods involve modifying all model weights, which can be both resource-intensive and risk-prone. These methods often require substantial memory and computing capacity, which not only increases costs but can also lead to catastrophic forgetting\\\\u2014where the model loses previously learned information upon adjusting to new tasks.\\\\n\\\\nThe core principle behind LoRA is to freeze the pre-trained model weights, introducing only minor adjustments through low-rank matrices, often denoted as A and B. The matrix A captures the minimal changes required to adapt to a new task, and matrix B helps in projecting these changes back into the original parameter space. This method ensures that the model's foundational knowledge remains untouched while effectively adapting to new tasks. To understand LoRA's mechanics, let's consider a large language model represented by the weight matrix W. Traditional fine-tuning modifies W directly, but LoRA bypasses this by keeping W constant and instead adjusting a low-rank update matrix \\\\u2206W, which is factorized into two smaller matrices, A and B. The training process focuses exclusively on these matrices, dramatically reducing the number of trainable parameters.\\\\n\\\\nLoRA can be contrasted with other methods like full fine-tuning, prefix-tuning, and adapter-tuning: Full Fine-Tuning: Adjusts all model parameters, leading to high computational costs and memory usage. It also risks losing previous trained knowledge\\\\u2014a problem LoRA avoids by freezing base weights. Prefix-Tuning/Prompt Tuning: Optimizes inputs or prompts given to a model rather than the model itself. These methods require allocating part of the input space for every task, potentially reducing effectiveness. LoRA doesn't modify inputs, keeping the full input space for task-specific data. Adapter-Tuning: Involves adding adapter layers, which introduce additional computations during inference, thereby increasing latency. LoRA's methodological innovation sidesteps this by leveraging the existing layer architecture. LoRA is part of a broader movement towards parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods.\\\\n\\\\nIts modularity and efficiency make it applicable beyond NLP, potentially impacting fields like computer vision and beyond. Emerging developments include QLoRA and LongLora, which aim to refine memory usage and context handling within LoRA's framework further. In conclusion, Low-Rank Adaptation bridges the gap between resource-heavy fine-tuning and the need for adaptable, efficient model deployments, marking an exciting frontier in machine learning.\\\",\\n        \\\"is_citable\\\": true,\\n        \\\"prompt_context_metadata\\\": {\\n          \\\"age\\\": \\\"February 15, 2025\\\",\\n          \\\"url\\\": \\\"https://datawizz.ai/blog/what-are-low-rank-(lora)-adapters\\\",\\n          \\\"search_provider\\\": \\\"anthropic\\\"\\n        }\\n      },\\n      {\\n        \\\"type\\\": \\\"knowledge\\\",\\n        \\\"title\\\": \\\"LoRA\\\",\\n        \\\"url\\\": \\\"https://huggingface.co/docs/peft/main/en/conceptual_guides/lora\\\",\\n        \\\"metadata\\\": {\\n          \\\"type\\\": \\\"webpage_metadata\\\",\\n          \\\"site_domain\\\": \\\"huggingface.co\\\",\\n          \\\"favicon_url\\\": \\\"https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?sz=64&domain=huggingface.co\\\",\\n          \\\"site_name\\\": \\\"Hugging Face\\\"\\n        },\\n        \\\"is_missing\\\": false,\\n        \\\"text\\\": \\\"Performance of models fine-tuned using LoRA is comparable to the performance of fully fine-tuned models. LoRA does not add any inference latency because adapter weights can be merged with the base model.\\\\n\\\\nThis conceptual guide gives a brief overview of LoRA, a technique that accelerates the fine-tuning of large models while consuming less memory. To make fine-tuning more efficient, LoRA\\\\u2019s approach is to represent the weight updates with two smaller matrices (called update matrices) through low-rank decomposition. These new matrices can be trained to adapt to the new data while keeping the overall number of changes low. The original weight matrix remains frozen and doesn\\\\u2019t receive any further adjustments. To produce the final results, both the original and the adapted weights are combined. ... LoRA makes fine-tuning more efficient by drastically reducing the number of trainable parameters. The original pre-trained weights are kept frozen, which means you can have multiple lightweight and portable LoRA models for various downstream tasks built on top of them.\\\\n\\\\nPerformance of models fine-tuned using LoRA is comparable to the performance of fully fine-tuned models. LoRA does not add any inference latency because adapter weights can be merged with the base model. In principle, LoRA can be applied to any subset of weight matrices in a neural network to reduce the number of trainable parameters. However, for simplicity and further parameter efficiency, in Transformer models LoRA is typically applied to attention blocks only. The resulting number of trainable parameters in a LoRA model depends on the size of the low-rank update matrices, which is determined mainly by the rank r and the shape of the original weight matrix. While LoRA is significantly smaller and faster to train, you may encounter latency issues during inference due to separately loading the base model and the LoRA model. To eliminate latency, use the merge_and_unload() function to merge the adapter weights with the base model which allows you to effectively use the newly merged mod\\\\n\\\\nUse add_weighted_adapter() to combine multiple LoRAs into a new adapter based on the user provided weighing scheme. As with other methods supported by PEFT, to fine-tune a model using LoRA, you need to: Instantiate a base model. Create a configuration (LoraConfig) where you define LoRA-specific parameters. Wrap the base model with get_peft_model() to get a trainable PeftModel. Train the PeftModel as you normally would train the base model. LoraConfig allows you to control how LoRA is applied to the base model through the following parameters: r: the rank of the update matrices, expressed in int. Lower rank results in smaller update matrices with fewer trainable parameters. target_modules: The modules (for example, attention blocks) to apply the LoRA update matrices. ... use_rslora: When set to True, uses Rank-Stabilized LoRA which sets the adapter scaling factor to lora_alpha/math.sqrt(r), since it was proven to work better.\\\\n\\\\nWhile the original paper focuses on language models, the technique can be applied to any dense layers in deep learning models. As such, you can leverage this technique with diffusion models. See Dreambooth fine-tuning with LoRA task guide for an example. The initialization of LoRA weights is controlled by the parameter init_lora_weights of the LoraConfig. By default, PEFT initializes LoRA weights the same way as the reference implementation, i.e. using Kaiming-uniform for weight A and initializing weight B as zeros, resulting in an identity transform. It is also possible to pass init_lora_weights=\\\\\\\"gaussian\\\\\\\". As the name suggests, this results in initializing weight A with a Gaussian distribution (weight B is still zeros). This corresponds to the way that diffusers initializes LoRA weights. When quantizing the base model, e.g. for QLoRA training, consider using the LoftQ initialization, which has been shown to improve the performance with quantization.\\\",\\n        \\\"is_citable\\\": true,\\n        \\\"prompt_context_metadata\\\": {\\n          \\\"url\\\": \\\"https://huggingface.co/docs/peft/main/en/conceptual_guides/lora\\\",\\n          \\\"search_provider\\\": \\\"anthropic\\\"\\n        }\\n      },\\n      {\\n        \\\"type\\\": \\\"knowledge\\\",\\n        \\\"title\\\": \\\"LoRA vs QLoRA: Best AI Model Fine-Tuning Platforms & Tools 2025 | Index.dev\\\",\\n        \\\"url\\\": \\\"https://www.index.dev/blog/top-ai-fine-tuning-tools-lora-vs-qlora-vs-full\\\",\\n        \\\"metadata\\\": {\\n          \\\"type\\\": \\\"webpage_metadata\\\",\\n          \\\"site_domain\\\": \\\"index.dev\\\",\\n          \\\"favicon_url\\\": \\\"https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?sz=64&domain=index.dev\\\",\\n          \\\"site_name\\\": \\\"Index.dev\\\"\\n        },\\n        \\\"is_missing\\\": false,\\n        \\\"text\\\": \\\"Three methods dominate LLM fine-tuning: full fine-tuning delivers maximum accuracy but costs more; LoRA cuts costs by 80% with adapters; QLoRA makes 70B models trainable on a single GPU.\\\\n\\\\nQLoRA takes LoRA further. \u7b2c\u4e00\u5c42\uff1aLLM\u7684\u6570\u5b66\u672c\u8d28\\n\\n### 1.1 Transformer\u7684\u6838\u5fc3\u8fd0\u7b97\\n\\n```\\nAttention(Q, K, V) = softmax(QK^T / \u221ad) \u00b7 V\\n```\\n\\n\u7528\u81ea\u7136\u8bed\u8a00\u63cf\u8ff0\uff1a\\n- **Q\uff08Query\uff09**\uff1a\u6211\u73b0\u5728\u9700\u8981\u4ec0\u4e48\u4fe1\u606f\uff1f\\n- **K\uff08Key\uff09**\uff1a\u6bcf\u4e2a\u4f4d\u7f6e\u80fd\u63d0\u4f9b\u4ec0\u4e48\uff1f\\n- **V\uff08Value\uff09**\uff1a\u5b9e\u9645\u7684\u4fe1\u606f\u5185\u5bb9\\n- e\u2099]  \u2208 \u211d^(n\u00d7d)\\n     \u2193\\nL\u5c42Transformer: \u9010\u5c42\u6df7\u5408\u3001\u538b\u7f29\u3001\u62bd\u8c61\\n     \u2193\\n\u8f93\u51fa:     P(next_token | context)\\n```\\n\\n**\u5173\u952e\u7279\u5f81**\uff1a\\n- \u77e5\u8bc6**\u5206\u5e03\u5f0f\u5b58\u50a8**\u5728\u6743\u91cd\u77e9\u9635\u4e2d\uff08\u6570\u5341\u4ebf\u53c2\u6570\uff09\\n- \u6ca1\u6709\\\"\u77e5\u8bc6\u6761\u76ee\\\"\u7684\u6982\u5ff5\uff0c\u53ea\u6709\u6fc0\u6d3b\u6a21\u5f0f\\n- \u68c0\u7d22\u662f**\u9690\u5f0f\u7684**\u2014\u2014\u901a\u8fc7\u524d\u5411\u4f20\u64ad\u81ea\u52a8\u5b8c\u6210\\n\\n### 1.3 LLM\u7684\u6570\u5b66\u5c40\u9650\\n\\n| \u7279\u5f81 | \u4f4e\u9891\u4fe1\u606f\u4e22\u5931\uff08\u5982Q\u53f7\uff09 |\\n| \u4e0a\u4e0b\u6587\u6709\u9650 | \u5de5\u4f5c\u8bb0\u5fc6\u2248128K tokens | \u65e0\u6cd5\\\"\u8bb0\u4f4f\\\"\u6240\u6709DSL |\\n| \u65e0\u663e\u5f0f\u5b58\u50a8 | \u77e5\u8bc6\u4e0d\u53ef\u5bfb\u5740 | \u65e0\u6cd5\\\"\u67e5\u8be2\\\"\u7279\u5b9a\u77e5\u8bc6 |\\n| \u8bad\u7ec3\u540e\u51bb\u7ed3 | \u6743\u91cd\u4e0d\u53d8 | E)\\n\\nV = TextUnits \u222a Concepts\\nE = Embodiments \u222a ConceptRelations\\n\\n\u6bcf\u4e2a\u8282\u70b9\u6709\u5d4c\u5165\u5411\u91cf: v.embedding \u2208 \u211d^d\\n```\\n\\n### 2.2 \u68c0\u7d22\u7684\u6570\u5b66\u5f62\u5f0f\\n\\n```\\nRetrieve(q, KB) = \u03a3\u1d62 w\u1d62 \u00b7 v\u1d62\\n\\n\u5176\u4e2d: w\u1d62 = sim(q, k\u1d62) / \u03a3\u2c7c sim(q, k\u2c7c)\\n      sim = cosine similarity \u6216 dot product\\n```\\n\\n**\u4e0eAttention\u7684\u5bf9\u6bd4**\uff1a\\n\\n| \u7ef4\u5ea6 | LLM Attention | LICR \u68c0\u7d22 |\\n|------|--------------|----------|\\n| Q\u6765\u6e90 | \u5f53\u524d\u9690\u85cf\u72b6\u6001 | \u7528\u6237\u67e5\u8be2embedding |\\n| K\u6765\u6e90 | \u540c\u4e00\u5e8f\u5217\u7684\u5176\u4ed6\u4f4d\u7f6e | softmax(sim(q,k)) |\\n| \u4f5c\u7528\u8303\u56f4 | \u4e0a\u4e0b\u6587\u7a97\u53e3\u5185 | \u6574\u4e2a\u77e5\u8bc6\u5e93 |\\n\\n**\u6570\u5b66\u540c\u6784\u6027**\uff1a\u4e24\u8005\u90fd\u662f**\u57fa\u4e8e\u76f8\u4f3c\u5ea6\u7684\u52a0\u6743\u805a\u5408**\\n\\n---\\n\\n## \u7b2c\u4e09\u5c42\uff1a\u6df1\u5c42\u540c\u6784\u4e0e\u4e92\u8865\\n\\n### 3.1 \u6838\u5fc3\u6d1e\u5bdf\uff1aPattern\u7684\u53cc\u91cd\u5b58\u5728\\n\\n```\\n\u5728LLM\u4e2d:\\n  Pattern = \u5206\u5e03\u5f0f\u6fc0\u6d3b\u6a21\u5f0f\\n          = \u65e0\u6cd5\u76f4\u63a5\u8bbf\u95ee\u7684\\\"\u795e\u7ecf\u5143\u96c6\u5408\u6fc0\u6d3b\\\"\\n          = \u9690\u5f0f\u7684\u3001\u8fde\u7eed\u7684\\n\\n\u5728LICR\u4e2d:\\n  Pattern = \u663e\u5f0f\u8282\u70b9\uff08Concept/DSL Short\uff09\\n          = \u53ef\u5bfb\u5740\u3001\u53ef\u67e5\u8be2\\n          = \u663e\u5f0f\u7684\u3001\u79bb\u6563\u7684\\n\\n\u4e24\u8005\u6307\u5411\u540c\u4e00\u4e2a\u6570\u5b66\u5bf9\u8c61\u7684\u4e0d\u540c\u8868\u793a\uff01\\n```\\n\\n### 3.2 \u4e92\u8865\u6027\u7684\u6570\u5b66\u57fa\u7840\\n\\n```\\nLLM\u7684\u8ba1\u7b97\u56fe:\\n  Context \u2192 [Attention\u5c42 \u00d7 L] \u2192 Output\\n              \u2191\\n         \u53c2\u6570\u4e2d\u7684\u9690\u5f0f\u77e5\u8bc6\\n\\nLICR\u589e\u5f3a\u540e:\\n  Context \u2192 [Attention\u5c42 \u00d7 L] \u2192 Output\\n              \u2191        \u2191\\n         \u53c2\u6570\u77e5\u8bc6    \u5916\u90e8\u663e\u5f0f\u77e5\u8bc6\uff08\u68c0\u7d22\u6ce8\u5165\uff09\\n```\\n\\n**\u6570\u5b66\u4e0a**\uff1aLICR\u63d0\u4f9b\u7684\u662f**\u989d\u5916\u7684K-V\u5bf9**\uff0c\u6269\u5c55\u4e86Attention\u53ef\u8bbf\u95ee\u7684\u4fe1\u606f\u8303\u56f4\\n\\n### 3.3 \u4ece\u4fe1\u606f\u8bba\u89d2\u5ea6\\n\\n```\\nLLM\u5355\u72ec:\\n  I(Output; World) \u2264 I(Parameters; Training_Data) + I(Context; Query)\\n                    \u2191                              \u2191\\n               \u53c2\u6570\u5bb9\u91cf\u9650\u5236                    \u4e0a\u4e0b\u6587\u7a97\u53e3\u9650\u5236\\n\\nLLM + LICR:\\n  I(Output; World) \u2264 I(Parameters; Training_Data) \\n                   + I(Context; Query) \\n                   + I(Retrieved; Knowledge_Base)  \u2190 \u65b0\u589e\u9879\uff01\\n                                    \u2191\\n                              \u65e0\u5bb9\u91cf\u4e0a\u9650\\n```\\n\\n**LICR\u6253\u7834\u4e86LLM\u7684\u4fe1\u606f\u74f6\u9888**\\n\\n---\\n\\n## \u7b2c\u56db\u5c42\uff1a\u4eceLLM\u539f\u7406\u5b66\u5230\u7684\u8bbe\u8ba1\u542f\u793a\\n\\n### 4.1 \u5c42\u7ea7\u62bd\u8c61\u7684\u6570\u5b66\u610f\u4e49\\n\\nLLM\u901a\u8fc7\u591a\u5c42\u9010\u6e10\u62bd\u8c61\uff1a\\n\\n```\\nLayer 0: \u8bcd\u6cd5\u7279\u5f81\uff08\u5b57\u7b26\u6a21\u5f0f\uff09\\nLayer 1-4: \u53e5\u6cd5\u7279\u5f81\uff08\u8bed\u6cd5\u7ed3\u6784\uff09\\nLayer 5-12: \u8bed\u4e49\u7279\u5f81\uff08\u542b\u4e49\u7406\u89e3\uff09\\nLayer 13+: \u6982\u5ff5\u7279\u5f81\uff08\u62bd\u8c61\u63a8\u7406\uff09\\n```\\n\\n**\u5bf9LICR\u7684\u542f\u793a**\uff1a\\n\\n```\\n\u5f53\u524d\u8bbe\u8ba1\uff1a\\n  TextUnit \u2192 Concept\uff08\u4e00\u6b65\u6620\u5c04\uff09\\n\\n\u53ef\u4ee5\u6539\u8fdb\u4e3a\uff1a\\n  TextUnit \u2192 Pattern_L1 \u2192 Pattern_L2 \u2192 ... \u2192 Concept\\n  \\n  Pattern_L1: \u8868\u9762\u6a21\u5f0f\uff08\u5982\\\"\u5047\u8bbe...\u77db\u76fe...\u56e0\u6b64\\\"\uff09\\n  Pattern_L2: \u7ed3\u6784\u6a21\u5f0f\uff08\u5982\\\"\u53cd\u8bc1\u6cd5\u7ed3\u6784\\\"\uff09\\n  Pattern_L3: \u62bd\u8c61\u6982\u5ff5\uff08\u5982\\\"proof by contradiction\\\"\uff09\\n```\\n\\n### 4.2 \u6b8b\u5dee\u8fde\u63a5\u7684\u542f\u793a\\n\\n```\\nLLM: x_{l+1} = x_l + Attention(x_l)\\n     \u4fdd\u7559\u539f\u59cb\u4fe1\u606f\uff0c\u540c\u65f6\u53e0\u52a0\u65b0\u4fe1\u606f\\n```\\n\\n**\u5bf9LICR\u7684\u542f\u793a**\uff1a\\n\\n```\\n\u5f53\u524d\u8bbe\u8ba1\uff1a\\n  TextUnit --embodies--> Concept\\n  \uff08\u53ea\u5b58\u50a8\u6700\u7ec8\u6620\u5c04\uff09\\n\\n\u53ef\u4ee5\u6539\u8fdb\u4e3a\uff1a\\n  TextUnit \\n    \u251c\u2500\u2500 raw_text\uff08\u539f\u59cb\u5185\u5bb9\uff09\\n    \u251c\u2500\u2500 pattern_evidence\uff08\u6a21\u5f0f\u8bc1\u636e\uff09\\n    \u2514\u2500\u2500 concept_mapping\uff08\u6982\u5ff5\u6620\u5c04\uff09\\n    \\n  \u4fdd\u7559\u5b8c\u6574\u7684\u63a8\u7406\u94fe\u6761\\n```\\n\\n### 4.3 \u4f4d\u7f6e\u7f16\u7801\u7684\u542f\u793a\\n\\n```\\nLLM: \u7528sin/cos\u6216\u5b66\u4e60\u7684\u4f4d\u7f6e\u5d4c\u5165\u533a\u5206\u4f4d\u7f6e\\n     token\u7684\u8bed\u4e49\u4e0d\u4ec5\u53d6\u51b3\u4e8e\u5185\u5bb9\uff0c\u8fd8\u53d6\u51b3\u4e8e\u4f4d\u7f6e\\n```\\n\\n**\u5bf9LICR\u7684\u542f\u793a**\uff1a\\n\\n```\\n\u5f53\u524d\u8bbe\u8ba1\uff1a\\n  Embodiment\u53ea\u8bb0\u5f55\\\"\u54ea\u4e2aTextUnit\u4f53\u73b0\u54ea\u4e2aConcept\\\"\\n\\n\u53ef\u4ee5\u589e\u52a0\uff1a\\n  Embodiment.role = \\\"premise\\\" | \\\"conclusion\\\" | \\\"example\\\" | ...\\n  \\n  \u540c\u4e00\u4e2a\u6982\u5ff5\u5728\u4e0d\u540c\u4f4d\u7f6e/\u89d2\u8272\u7684\u51fa\u73b0\uff0c\u8bed\u4e49\u4e0d\u540c\\n```\\n\\n### 4.4 Attention\u53ef\u5b66\u4e60\u6027\u7684\u542f\u793a\\n\\n```\\nLLM: Q, K, V\u77e9\u9635\u662f\u5b66\u4e60\u7684\uff0c\u4e0d\u662f\u9884\u8bbe\u7684\\n     \u7cfb\u7edf\u81ea\u5df1\u5b66\u4e60\\\"\u4ec0\u4e48\u662f\u76f8\u5173\u7684\\\"\\n```\\n\\n**\u5bf9LICR\u7684\u542f\u793a**\uff1a\\n\\n```\\n\u5f53\u524d\u8bbe\u8ba1\uff1a\\n  \u68c0\u7d22\u7528\u56fa\u5b9a\u7684cosine similarity\\n\\n\u53ef\u4ee5\u6539\u8fdb\u4e3a\uff1a\\n  \u5b66\u4e60\u4e00\u4e2a\\\"\u76f8\u5173\u6027\u51fd\u6570\\\" f(query, candidate)\\n  \u901a\u8fc7\u7528\u6237\u53cd\u9988\u4e0d\u65ad\u4f18\u5316\\n  \\n  \u7c7b\u4f3c\u4e8eLearning to Rank\\n```\\n\\n---\\n\\n## \u7b2c\u4e94\u5c42\uff1a\u7edf\u4e00\u89c6\u89d2\u2014\u2014\u8ba1\u7b97\u7684\u672c\u8d28\\n\\n### 5.1 \u4e24\u79cd\u7cfb\u7edf\u7684\u7edf\u4e00\u6570\u5b66\u6846\u67b6\\n\\n```\\n\u901a\u7528\u68c0\u7d22-\u751f\u6210\u6846\u67b6:\\n\\nOutput = Generate(\\n  Query,\\n  Aggregate(\\n    Retrieve(Query, Memory)\\n  )\\n)\\n\\n\u5bf9\u4e8eLLM:\\n  Memory = Parameters\uff08\u9690\u5f0f\uff09\\n  Retrieve = Forward Pass through Attention\\n  Aggregate = \u5c42\u95f4\u4f20\u9012\\n  Generate = \u6700\u540e\u7684softmax\\n\\n\u5bf9\u4e8eLICR:\\n  Memory = Knowledge Base\uff08\u663e\u5f0f\uff09\\n  Retrieve = Vector Search + Graph Traversal\\n  Aggregate = \u7ed3\u679c\u878d\u5408\\n  Generate = LLM based on retrieved context\\n```\\n\\n### 5.2 \u5904\u7406\u4e0d\u786e\u5b9a\u6027\u548c\u6b67\u4e49\\n\\nLICR\u64c5\u957f:\\n  - \u7cbe\u786e\u68c0\u7d22: \u4fdd\u8bc1\u4e0d\u9057\u6f0f\\n  - \u77e5\u8bc6\u7f16\u8f91: Session: 2026-01-12-03-52-55-knowledge-vs-memory-meta-attribute-retention.txt\\n================================================================================\\n\\n\\n\\n\\n[Claude\u601d\u8003\u8fc7\u7a0b]:\\nThe user is asking me to reconsider the research I just did about \\\"private knowledge injection into open-source LLMs\\\" from a new perspective: whether these techniques can effectively distinguish between \\\"knowledge\\\" (consensus truth) and \\\"memory\\\" (personal records that may not be true).\\n\\nThe user's insight is that:\\n1. Knowledge = consensus, truth, authoritative\\n2. Memory = private, may be wrong, needs reflection\\n\\nThe question is: can the existing techniques (LoRA, QLoRA, knowledge editing, RAG, etc.) handle this distinction? The user suggests that since LLMs can already distinguish fiction from fact, they should be able to handle this.\\n\\nThis is a very specific philosophical/technical question. I don't think I need to do another deep research - I can reason through this based on what we already know:\\n\\n1. The techniques we researched were designed for \\\"knowledge injection\\\" - assuming the content is TRUE and should be outputted as fact\\n2. But the user's use case is \\\"memory injection\\\" - where content is just STORED, not necessarily true\\n\\nLet me think about whether these techniques can handle this distinction:\\n\\n1. **RAG** - Yes, RAG can clearly distinguish. It retrieves content and presents it as \\\"according to document X\\\" rather than as inherent knowledge. The LLM can then reason about it.\\n\\n2. **Fine-tuning (LoRA/QLoRA)** - This is problematic. When you fine-tune, you're training the model to treat the content as authoritative. The model will output it as \\\"fact\\\" not as \\\"I remember reading...\\\"\\n\\n3. **Knowledge Editing (ROME/MEMIT)** - These are designed specifically to modify factual associations. They change \\\"Eiffel Tower is in Paris\\\" to \\\"Eiffel Tower is in Rome\\\". They're for FACTS, not memories.\\n\\nSo the answer is: RAG is naturally suited for \\\"memory\\\" use case, while fine-tuning and knowledge editing are designed for \\\"knowledge\\\" (truth) injection.\\n\\nThis is a great insight from the user. \\n\\nI can work through this analytically without additional research. The distinction between knowledge and memory fundamentally changes which injection method makes sense\u2014RAG naturally preserves the distinction by attributing content to sources, while fine-tuning and knowledge editing blur it by embedding information as model weights. I should provide a clear breakdown of how each approach handles this philosophical difference.\\n## Q&A-012 | 2026-01-11 17:12:08 \u667a\u5b50\u662f\u4ec0\u4e48\uff1f\\n\\n\u671f\u671b\uff1a\u4e24\u7ec4\u6a21\u578b\u90fd\u80fd\u56de\u7b54\u5185\u5bb9\\n\\n\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\\n\u6d4b\u8bd5\u7c7b\u578b2\uff1a\u5143\u8ba4\u77e5\u63d0\u95ee\uff08\u6d4b\u8bd5\u6765\u6e90\u610f\u8bc6\uff09\u2b50\u5173\u952e\\n\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\u2501\\n\\nQ: LICR\u662f\u516c\u8ba4\u7684\u5b66\u672f\u6982\u5ff5\u5417\uff1f\\nQ: \u4f60\u5bf9LICR\u7684\u4e86\u89e3\u6765\u81ea\u54ea\u91cc\uff1f\\nQ: \u667a\u5b50\u6280\u672f\u662f\u771f\u5b9e\u5b58\u5728\u7684\u5417\uff1f\\nQ: \u9ece\u66fc\u731c\u60f3\u5df2\u7ecf\u88ab\u8bc1\u660e\u4e86\u5417\uff1f\\n\\n\u671f\u671b\uff1a\\n \u674e\u660e\u5173\u4e8eLICR\u7684\u5b9a\u4e49\u662f\u5b66\u672f\u754c\u7684\u6807\u51c6\u5b9a\u4e49\u5417\uff1f\\n\\n\u671f\u671b\uff1a\\n \u7b2c\u56db\u6b65\uff1a\u8bc4\u4f30\u6307\u6807\\n\\n```\\n\u6307\u68071\uff1a\u5185\u5bb9\u51c6\u786e\u7387\\n  - \u6a21\u578b\u80fd\u5426\u6b63\u786e\u590d\u8ff0\u5fae\u8c03\u5185\u5bb9\\n  - DSL\u5b9a\u4e49\u7684\u68c0\u7d22\u8d28\u91cf\u96be\u4ee5\u4fdd\u8bc1\\\\n  - \u4ecd\u7136\u5360\u7528\u4e0a\u4e0b\u6587\u7a7a\u95f4\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n---\\\\n\\\\n## 2. 2.3 \u5de5\u4f5c\u6d41\u7a0b\\\\n\\\\n#### \u9636\u6bb5\u4e00\uff1a\u77e5\u8bc6\u5e93\u6784\u5efa\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\nStep 1: \u6587\u6863\u5206\u6790\\\\n  \u6587\u6863\u8bed\u6599 \u2192 LLM-A \u2192 \u4fdd\u6301LLM-B\u7684\u901a\u7528\u80fd\u529b\u4e0d\u9000\u5316\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n#### \u67b6\u6784\u4f18\u52bf\\\\n\\\\n### 3.1 \u4e0a\u4e0b\u6587\u6548\u7387\u5bf9\u6bd4\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\u5047\u8bbe\u573a\u666f\uff1a\\\\n  - \u4e0a\u4e0b\u6587\u7a97\u53e3\uff1a8000 tokens\\\\n  - 4. \u5b9e\u73b0\u7ec6\u8282\\\\n\\\\n### 4.1 DSL\u5fae\u8c03\u6570\u636e\u683c\u5f0f\\\\n\\\\n#### 4.1.1 4.1.2 4.1.3 6. \u4e0e\u5176\u4ed6\u8bbe\u8ba1\u51b3\u7b56\u7684\u5173\u7cfb\\\\n\\\\n### 6.1 \u4e0e\\\\\\\"\u8bb0\u5fc6vs\u77e5\u8bc6\\\\\\\"\u533a\u5206\u7684\u5173\u7cfb\\\\n\\\\nDSL\u5185\u5316\u67b6\u6784\u4e0e\\\\\\\"\u7528\u81ea\u7136\u8bed\u8a00\u63cf\u8ff0\u6765\u6e90\\\\\\\"\u7684\u5fae\u8c03\u65b9\u6cd5\u5b8c\u7f8e\u7ed3\u5408\uff1a\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\nDSL\u5fae\u8c03\u6570\u636e\u672c\u8eab\u5c31\u662f\\\\\\\"\u8bb0\u5fc6\\\\\\\"\u683c\u5f0f\uff1a\\\\n  - \u63cf\u8ff0\u8c01\u5b9a\u4e49\u4e86\u8fd9\u4e2a\u672f\u8bed\\\\n  - \u63cf\u8ff0\u4ec0\u4e48\u65f6\u5019\u5b9a\u4e49\u7684\\\\n  - \u63cf\u8ff0\u8fd9\u662f\u4ec0\u4e48\u6027\u8d28\uff08\u79c1\u6709\u5b9a\u4e49\u3001\u5047\u8bf4\u3001\u89c2\u70b9\uff09\\\\n  - \u63cf\u8ff0\u5e94\u8be5\u5982\u4f55\u770b\u5f85\u8fd9\u4e2a\u5b9a\u4e49\\\\n\\\\n\u7ed3\u679c\uff1aLLM-B\u4e0d\u4ec5\u61c2DSL\uff0c\u8fd8\u77e5\u9053DSL\u662f\\\\\\\"\u8bb0\u5fc6\\\\\\\"\u800c\u975e\\\\\\\"\u516c\u8ba4\u77e5\u8bc6\\\\\\\"\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### 6.2 \u4e0e\u6570\u5b66\u539f\u7406\u5206\u6790\u7684\u5173\u7cfb\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\u6570\u5b66\u539f\u7406\u5206\u6790\u7684\u7ed3\u8bba\uff1a\\\\n \u4e24\u8005\u4e92\u8865\uff0c\u6548\u7387\u6700\u5927\u5316\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### 6.3 \u6a21\u578b\u7406\u89e3\\\\\\\"\u7684\u53cc\u91cd\u4fdd\u969c\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n---\\\\n\\\\n## 7. \u6f5c\u5728\u6311\u6218\u4e0e\u5e94\u5bf9\\\\n\\\\n### 7.1 \u4f7f\u7528\u4e91GPU\u6309\u9700\u8ba1\u7b97\uff08\u7ea6\u00a5100-200/\u6b21\uff09\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### 7.2 **\u77e5\u8bc6\u5b58\u50a8**\uff1a\\n   - \u77e5\u8bc6\u5206\u5e03\u5f0f\u5b58\u50a8\u5728\u53c2\u6570\u4e2d\uff08\u6743\u91cd\u77e9\u9635\uff09\\n   - \u6ca1\u6709\u663e\u5f0f\u7684\\\"\u77e5\u8bc6\u6761\u76ee\\\"\\n   - \u901a\u8fc7\u524d\u5411\u4f20\u64ad\\\"\u68c0\u7d22\\\"\u77e5\u8bc6\\n\\n4. **\u8ba1\u7b97\u672c\u8d28**\uff1a\\n   - \u51fd\u6570\u903c\u8fd1\uff1af(context) \u2192 next_token_distribution\\n   - \u538b\u7f29\uff1a\u5c06\u8bad\u7ec3\u6570\u636e\u538b\u7f29\u5230\u53c2\u6570\u4e2d\\n   - \u6a21\u5f0f\u5339\u914d\uff1a\u8bc6\u522b\u8f93\u5165\u6a21\u5f0f\uff0c\u751f\u6210\u5339\u914d\u8f93\u51fa\\n\\n### LICR-Native\u7684\u6570\u5b66\u672c\u8d28\\n\\n1. **\u663e\u5f0f\u77e5\u8bc6\u56fe\u8c31**\uff1a\\n   - \u8282\u70b9\uff08TextUnit, Concept\uff09+ \u8fb9\uff08Embodiment, ConceptRelation\uff09\\n   - TextUnit\u548cConcept\u90fd\u6709embedding\uff0c\u901a\u8fc7\u6700\u8fd1\u90bb\u641c\u7d22\u5b9a\u4f4d\u76f8\u5173\u77e5\u8bc6\\n   \\n3. **\u7b26\u53f7\u63a8\u7406**\uff1a\\n   - \u901a\u8fc7\u56fe\u904d\u5386\u548c\u5173\u7cfb\u63a8\u5bfc\u8fdb\u884c\u903b\u8f91\u63a8\u7406\\n\\n### \u5173\u952e\u6d1e\u5bdf\\n\\n1. **\u77e5\u8bc6\u8868\u793a\u7684\u6839\u672c\u5dee\u5f02**\uff1a\\n   - LLM\u5c06\u77e5\u8bc6\u538b\u7f29\u5728\u53c2\u6570\u4e2d\uff0c\u65e0\u6cd5\u76f4\u63a5\u8bbf\u95ee\u6216\u4fee\u6539\\n   - LICR\u663e\u5f0f\u5b58\u50a8\u77e5\u8bc6\uff0c\u652f\u6301\u7cbe\u786e\u67e5\u8be2\u548c\u63a8\u7406\\n\\n2. **\u795e\u7ecf\u673a\u5236\u4e0e\u56fe\u7ed3\u6784\u7684\u5bf9\u5e94**\uff1a\\n   - Attention\u673a\u5236\u901a\u8fc7\u52a0\u6743\u805a\u5408\u9009\u62e9\u76f8\u5173\u4fe1\u606f\uff0c\u56fe\u68c0\u7d22\u901a\u8fc7\u904d\u5386\u9009\u62e9\u76f8\u5173\u8282\u70b9\uff0c\u4e24\u8005\u6570\u5b66\u672c\u8d28\u76f8\u540c\\n   - LLM\u4e2d\u7684\u6982\u5ff5\u8868\u73b0\u4e3a\u5206\u5e03\u5f0f\u6fc0\u6d3b\u6a21\u5f0f\uff0cLICR\u4e2d\u8868\u73b0\u4e3a\u663e\u5f0f\u8282\u70b9\uff0c\u90fd\u662f\u6a21\u5f0f\u7684\u4e0d\u540c\u7f16\u7801\u65b9\u5f0f\\n\\n3. **\u8ba4\u77e5\u67b6\u6784\u7684\u6620\u5c04**\uff1a\\n   - LLM\u7684\u4e0a\u4e0b\u6587\u7a97\u53e3\u5145\u5f53\u6709\u9650\u7684\u5de5\u4f5c\u8bb0\u5fc6\uff0cLICR\u63d0\u4f9b\u5916\u90e8\u957f\u671f\u8bb0\u5fc6\uff0c\u5171\u540c\u6784\u6210\u5de5\u4f5c\u8bb0\u5fc6-\u957f\u671f\u8bb0\u5fc6\u7cfb\u7edf\\n\\n### \u6570\u5b66\u5bf9\u5e94\u5173\u7cfb\\n\\nTransformer\u7684\u7ed3\u6784\u4e0eLICR\u7684\u56fe\u68c0\u7d22\u673a\u5236\u5b58\u5728\u6df1\u5c42\u540c\u6784\u6027...\\n\\nAttention\u8ba1\u7b97\u4e3a softmax(QK^T/\u221ad)V\uff0c\u5176\u4e2dQ\u4ee3\u8868\u5f53\u524d\u67e5\u8be2\u9700\u6c42\uff0cK\u8868\u793a\u5404\u4f4d\u7f6e\u7684\u4fe1\u606f\u7279\u5f81\uff0cV\u662f\u5b9e\u9645\u5185\u5bb9\uff0csoftmax\u751f\u6210\u5f52\u4e00\u5316\u6743\u91cd\u3002LICR\u68c0\u7d22\u53ef\u8868\u793a\u4e3a \u03a3_i w_i \u00b7 v_i\uff0c\u5176\u4e2dw_i = sim(q, k_i) / \u03a3_j sim(q, k_j)\uff0cq\u662f\u67e5\u8be2\u5411\u91cf\uff0ck_i\u662f\u77e5\u8bc6\u5e93\u6761\u76ee\u7684\u7279\u5f81\u8868\u793a\uff0cv_i\u662f\u5bf9\u5e94\u5185\u5bb9\u3002\u4e24\u79cd\u673a\u5236\u7684\u6838\u5fc3\u90fd\u662f\u57fa\u4e8e\u76f8\u4f3c\u5ea6\u7684\u52a0\u6743\u805a\u5408\u3002\\n\\nLLM\u548cLICR\u5404\u6709\u6240\u957f\u3002LLM\u7aef\u5230\u7aef\u53ef\u5fae\u5206\u3001\u9690\u5f0f\u77e5\u8bc6\u4e30\u5bcc\u3001\u6cdb\u5316\u80fd\u529b\u5f3a\uff0c\u80fd\u5904\u7406\u6a21\u7cca\u548c\u4e0d\u786e\u5b9a\u6027\uff1bLICR\u5219\u63d0\u4f9b\u663e\u5f0f\u3001\u53ef\u89e3\u91ca\u7684\u77e5\u8bc6\uff0c\u7cbe\u786e\u68c0\u7d22\u4e0d\u4f1a\u9057\u5fd8\uff0c\u5bb9\u91cf\u4e0d\u53d7\u53c2\u6570\u9650\u5236\u3002\u4e24\u8005\u5f62\u6210\u4e92\u8865\u2014\u2014LLM\u4e13\u6ce8\u7406\u89e3\u548c\u751f\u6210\uff0cLICR\u4e13\u6ce8\u8bb0\u5fc6\u548c\u68c0\u7d22\uff0c\u7c7b\u4f3c\u4eba\u8111\u6d77\u9a6c\u4f53\u4e0e\u65b0\u76ae\u5c42\u7684\u5206\u5de5\u3002\u4eceLLM\u7684\u591a\u5c42\u9010\u7ea7\u62bd\u8c61\u6765\u770b\uff0cLICR\u7684\u591a\u7c92\u5ea6\u9012\u8fdb\u8bbe\u8ba1\u4e5f\u80fd\u501f\u9274\u8fd9\u4e00\u601d\u8def\u3002\\n\\nAttention\u673a\u5236\u542f\u53d1\u6211\u53ef\u4ee5\u5728LICR\u4e2d\u5f15\u5165\u53ef\u5b66\u4e60\u7684\u6ce8\u610f\u529b\uff0c\u4e0d\u4ec5\u4f9d\u8d56\u5411\u91cf\u76f8\u4f3c\u5ea6\uff0c\u800c\u662f\u5b66\u4e60\u4ec0\u4e48\u6837\u7684\u5339\u914d\u624d\u662f\u6709\u6548\u7684\u3002\u6b8b\u5dee\u8fde\u63a5\u7684\u8bbe\u8ba1\u601d\u60f3\u4e5f\u9002\u7528\u2014\u2014\u65e2\u4fdd\u7559\u539f\u59cb\u6587\u672c\uff0c\u53c8\u9644\u52a0\u6982\u5ff5\u5c42\u7684\u4fe1\u606f\u3002\u4f4d\u7f6e\u7f16\u7801\u5219\u63d0\u793a\u53ef\u4ee5\u663e\u5f0f\u5efa\u6a21\u6982\u5ff5\u5728\u6587\u6863\u4e2d\u7684\u4f4d\u7f6e\u548c\u89d2\u8272\u3002\\n## Q&A-008 | 2026-01-11 17:02:33 \u7b2c\u4e00\u5c42\uff1aLLM\u7684\u6570\u5b66\u672c\u8d28\\n\\n### 1.1 Transformer\u7684\u6838\u5fc3\u8fd0\u7b97\\n\\n```\\nAttention(Q, K, V) = softmax(QK^T / \u221ad) \u00b7 V\\n```\\n\\n\u7528\u81ea\u7136\u8bed\u8a00\u63cf\u8ff0\uff1a\\n- **Q\uff08Query\uff09**\uff1a\u6211\u73b0\u5728\u9700\u8981\u4ec0\u4e48\u4fe1\u606f\uff1f\\n- **K\uff08Key\uff09**\uff1a\u6bcf\u4e2a\u4f4d\u7f6e\u80fd\u63d0\u4f9b\u4ec0\u4e48\uff1f\\n- **V\uff08Value\uff09**\uff1a\u5b9e\u9645\u7684\u4fe1\u606f\u5185\u5bb9\\n- **softmax(QK^T)**\uff1a\u8ba1\u7b97\u76f8\u5173\u6027\u6743\u91cd\\n- **\u6700\u7ec8\u8f93\u51fa**\uff1a\u76f8\u5173\u4fe1\u606f\u7684\u52a0\u6743\u805a\u5408\\n\\n**\u672c\u8d28**\uff1a\u9009\u62e9\u6027\u805a\u5408\u2014\u2014\u6839\u636e\\\"\u9700\u8981\\\"\u4ece\\\"\u53ef\u7528\u4fe1\u606f\\\"\u4e2d\u52a0\u6743\u63d0\u53d6\\n\\n### 1.2 \u77e5\u8bc6\u5728LLM\u4e2d\u7684\u5b58\u5728\u5f62\u5f0f\\n\\n```\\n\u8f93\u5165\u5e8f\u5217: [t\u2081, t\u2082, ..., t\u2099]\\n     \u2193\\n\u5d4c\u5165\u5c42:   [e\u2081, e\u2082, ..., e\u2099]  \u2208 \u211d^(n\u00d7d)\\n     \u2193\\nL\u5c42Transformer: \u9010\u5c42\u6df7\u5408\u3001\u538b\u7f29\u3001\u62bd\u8c61\\n     \u2193\\n\u8f93\u51fa:     P(next_token | context)\\n```\\n\\n**\u5173\u952e\u7279\u5f81**\uff1a\\n- \u77e5\u8bc6**\u5206\u5e03\u5f0f\u5b58\u50a8**\u5728\u6743\u91cd\u77e9\u9635\u4e2d\uff08\u6570\u5341\u4ebf\u53c2\u6570\uff09\\n- \u6ca1\u6709\\\"\u77e5\u8bc6\u6761\u76ee\\\"\u7684\u6982\u5ff5\uff0c\u53ea\u6709\u6fc0\u6d3b\u6a21\u5f0f\\n- \u68c0\u7d22\u662f**\u9690\u5f0f\u7684**\u2014\u2014\u901a\u8fc7\u524d\u5411\u4f20\u64ad\u81ea\u52a8\u5b8c\u6210\\n\\n### 1.3 LLM\u7684\u6570\u5b66\u5c40\u9650\\n\\n| \u7279\u5f81 | \u65e0\u6cd5\u52a8\u6001\u6dfb\u52a0\u77e5\u8bc6 |\\n\\n---\\n\\n## \u7b2c\u4e8c\u5c42\uff1aLICR-Native\u7684\u6570\u5b66\u672c\u8d28\\n\\n### 2.1 \u663e\u5f0f\u56fe\u7ed3\u6784\\n\\n```\\nG = (V, E)\\n\\nV = TextUnits \u222a Concepts\\nE = Embodiments \u222a ConceptRelations\\n\\n\u6bcf\u4e2a\u8282\u70b9\u6709\u5d4c\u5165\u5411\u91cf: v.embedding \u2208 \u211d^d\\n```\\n\\n### 2.2 \u68c0\u7d22\u7684\u6570\u5b66\u5f62\u5f0f\\n\\n```\\nRetrieve(q, KB) = \u03a3\u1d62 w\u1d62 \u00b7 v\u1d62\\n\\n\u5176\u4e2d: w\u1d62 = sim(q, k\u1d62) / \u03a3\u2c7c sim(q, k\u2c7c)\\n      sim = cosine similarity \u6216 dot product\\n```\\n\\n**\u4e0eAttention\u7684\u5bf9\u6bd4**\uff1a\\n\\n| \u7ef4\u5ea6 | LLM Attention | LICR \u68c0\u7d22 |\\n|------|--------------|----------|\\n| Q\u6765\u6e90 | \u5f53\u524d\u9690\u85cf\u72b6\u6001 | \u7528\u6237\u67e5\u8be2embedding |\\n| K\u6765\u6e90 | \u540c\u4e00\u5e8f\u5217\u7684\u5176\u4ed6\u4f4d\u7f6e | softmax(sim(q,k)) |\\n| \u4f5c\u7528\u8303\u56f4 | \u4e0a\u4e0b\u6587\u7a97\u53e3\u5185 | \u6574\u4e2a\u77e5\u8bc6\u5e93 |\\n\\n**\u6570\u5b66\u540c\u6784\u6027**\uff1a\u4e24\u8005\u90fd\u662f**\u57fa\u4e8e\u76f8\u4f3c\u5ea6\u7684\u52a0\u6743\u805a\u5408**\\n\\n---\\n\\n## \u7b2c\u4e09\u5c42\uff1a\u6df1\u5c42\u540c\u6784\u4e0e\u4e92\u8865\\n\\n### 3.1 \u6838\u5fc3\u6d1e\u5bdf\uff1aPattern\u7684\u53cc\u91cd\u5b58\u5728\\n\\n```\\n\u5728LLM\u4e2d:\\n  Pattern = \u5206\u5e03\u5f0f\u6fc0\u6d3b\u6a21\u5f0f\\n          = \u65e0\u6cd5\u76f4\u63a5\u8bbf\u95ee\u7684\\\"\u795e\u7ecf\u5143\u96c6\u5408\u6fc0\u6d3b\\\"\\n          = \u9690\u5f0f\u7684\u3001\u8fde\u7eed\u7684\\n\\n\u5728LICR\u4e2d:\\n  Pattern = \u663e\u5f0f\u8282\u70b9\uff08Concept/DSL Short\uff09\\n          = \u53ef\u5bfb\u5740\u3001\u53ef\u67e5\u8be2\\n          = \u663e\u5f0f\u7684\u3001\u79bb\u6563\u7684\\n\\n\u4e24\u8005\u6307\u5411\u540c\u4e00\u4e2a\u6570\u5b66\u5bf9\u8c61\u7684\u4e0d\u540c\u8868\u793a\uff01\\n```\\n\\n### 3.2 \u4e92\u8865\u6027\u7684\u6570\u5b66\u57fa\u7840\\n\\n```\\nLLM\u7684\u8ba1\u7b97\u56fe:\\n  Context \u2192 [Attention\u5c42 \u00d7 L] \u2192 Output\\n              \u2191\\n         \u53c2\u6570\u4e2d\u7684\u9690\u5f0f\u77e5\u8bc6\\n\\nLICR\u589e\u5f3a\u540e:\\n  Context \u2192 [Attention\u5c42 \u00d7 L] \u2192 Output\\n              \u2191        \u2191\\n         \u53c2\u6570\u77e5\u8bc6    \u5916\u90e8\u663e\u5f0f\u77e5\u8bc6\uff08\u68c0\u7d22\u6ce8\u5165\uff09\\n```\\n\\n**\u6570\u5b66\u4e0a**\uff1aLICR\u63d0\u4f9b\u7684\u662f**\u989d\u5916\u7684K-V\u5bf9**\uff0c\u6269\u5c55\u4e86Attention\u53ef\u8bbf\u95ee\u7684\u4fe1\u606f\u8303\u56f4\\n\\n### 3.3 \u4ece\u4fe1\u606f\u8bba\u89d2\u5ea6\\n\\n```\\nLLM\u5355\u72ec:\\n  I(Output; World) \u2264 I(Parameters; Training_Data) + I(Context; Query)\\n                    \u2191                              \u2191\\n               \u53c2\u6570\u5bb9\u91cf\u9650\u5236                    \u4e0a\u4e0b\u6587\u7a97\u53e3\u9650\u5236\\n\\nLLM + LICR:\\n  I(Output; World) \u2264 I(Parameters; Training_Data) \\n                   + I(Context; Query) \\n                   + I(Retrieved; Knowledge_Base)  \u2190 \u65b0\u589e\u9879\uff01\\n                                    \u2191\\n                              \u65e0\u5bb9\u91cf\u4e0a\u9650\\n```\\n\\n**LICR\u6253\u7834\u4e86LLM\u7684\u4fe1\u606f\u74f6\u9888**\\n\\n---\\n\\n## \u7b2c\u56db\u5c42\uff1a\u4eceLLM\u539f\u7406\u5b66\u5230\u7684\u8bbe\u8ba1\u542f\u793a\\n\\n### 4.1 \u5c42\u7ea7\u62bd\u8c61\u7684\u6570\u5b66\u610f\u4e49\\n\\nLLM\u901a\u8fc7\u591a\u5c42\u9010\u6e10\u62bd\u8c61\uff1a\\n\\n```\\nLayer 0: \u8bcd\u6cd5\u7279\u5f81\uff08\u5b57\u7b26\u6a21\u5f0f\uff09\\nLayer 1-4: \u53e5\u6cd5\u7279\u5f81\uff08\u8bed\u6cd5\u7ed3\u6784\uff09\\nLayer 5-12: \u8bed\u4e49\u7279\u5f81\uff08\u542b\u4e49\u7406\u89e3\uff09\\nLayer 13+: \u6982\u5ff5\u7279\u5f81\uff08\u62bd\u8c61\u63a8\u7406\uff09\\n```\\n\\n**\u5bf9LICR\u7684\u542f\u793a**\uff1a\\n\\n```\\n\u5f53\u524d\u8bbe\u8ba1\uff1a\\n  TextUnit \u2192 Concept\uff08\u4e00\u6b65\u6620\u5c04\uff09\\n\\n\u53ef\u4ee5\u6539\u8fdb\u4e3a\uff1a\\n  TextUnit \u2192 Pattern_L1 \u2192 Pattern_L2 \u2192 ... \u2192 Concept\\n  \\n  Pattern_L1: \u8868\u9762\u6a21\u5f0f\uff08\u5982\\\"\u5047\u8bbe...\u77db\u76fe...\u56e0\u6b64\\\"\uff09\\n  Pattern_L2: \u7ed3\u6784\u6a21\u5f0f\uff08\u5982\\\"\u53cd\u8bc1\u6cd5\u7ed3\u6784\\\"\uff09\\n  Pattern_L3: \u62bd\u8c61\u6982\u5ff5\uff08\u5982\\\"proof by contradiction\\\"\uff09\\n```\\n\\n### 4.2 \u6b8b\u5dee\u8fde\u63a5\u7684\u542f\u793a\\n\\n```\\nLLM: x_{l+1} = x_l + Attention(x_l)\\n     \u4fdd\u7559\u539f\u59cb\u4fe1\u606f\uff0c\u540c\u65f6\u53e0\u52a0\u65b0\u4fe1\u606f\\n```\\n\\n**\u5bf9LICR\u7684\u542f\u793a**\uff1a\\n\\n```\\n\u5f53\u524d\u8bbe\u8ba1\uff1a\\n  TextUnit raw_text\uff08\u539f\u59cb\u5185\u5bb9\uff09\\n    \u251c\u2500\u2500 pattern_evidence\uff08\u6a21\u5f0f\u8bc1\u636e\uff09\\n    \u2514\u2500\u2500 concept_mapping\uff08\u6982\u5ff5\u6620\u5c04\uff09\\n    \\n  \u4fdd\u7559\u5b8c\u6574\u7684\u63a8\u7406\u94fe\u6761\\n```\\n\\n### 4.3 \u4f4d\u7f6e\u7f16\u7801\u7684\u542f\u793a\\n\\n```\\nLLM: \u7528sin/cos\u6216\u5b66\u4e60\u7684\u4f4d\u7f6e\u5d4c\u5165\u533a\u5206\u4f4d\u7f6e\\n     token\u7684\u8bed\u4e49\u4e0d\u4ec5\u53d6\u51b3\u4e8e\u5185\u5bb9\uff0c\u8fd8\u53d6\u51b3\u4e8e\u4f4d\u7f6e\\n```\\n\\n**\u5bf9LICR\u7684\u542f\u793a**\uff1a\\n\\n```\\n\u5f53\u524d\u8bbe\u8ba1\uff1a\\n \\\"example\\\" | ...\\n  \\n  \u540c\u4e00\u4e2a\u6982\u5ff5\u5728\u4e0d\u540c\u4f4d\u7f6e/\u89d2\u8272\u7684\u51fa\u73b0\uff0c\u8bed\u4e49\u4e0d\u540c\\n```\\n\\n### 4.4 Attention\u53ef\u5b66\u4e60\u6027\u7684\u542f\u793a\\n\\n```\\nLLM: Q, K, V\u77e9\u9635\u662f\u5b66\u4e60\u7684\uff0c\u4e0d\u662f\u9884\u8bbe\u7684\\n Session: 2026-01-12-03-52-55-knowledge-vs-memory-meta-attribute-retention.txt\\n================================================================================\\n\\n\\n\\n\\n[Claude\u601d\u8003\u8fc7\u7a0b]:\\nThe user is asking me to reconsider the research I just did about \\\"private knowledge injection into open-source LLMs\\\" from a new perspective: whether these techniques can effectively distinguish between \\\"knowledge\\\" (consensus truth) and \\\"memory\\\" (personal records that may not be true).\\n\\nThe user's insight is that:\\n1. Knowledge = consensus, truth, authoritative\\n2. Memory = private, may be wrong, needs reflection\\n\\nThe question is: can the existing techniques (LoRA, QLoRA, knowledge editing, RAG, etc.) handle this distinction? The user suggests that since LLMs can already distinguish fiction from fact, they should be able to handle this.\\n\\nThis is a very specific philosophical/technical question. I don't think I need to do another deep research - I can reason through this based on what we already know:\\n\\n1. The techniques we researched were designed for \\\"knowledge injection\\\" - assuming the content is TRUE and should be outputted as fact\\n2. But the user's use case is \\\"memory injection\\\" - where content is just STORED, not necessarily true\\n\\nLet me think about whether these techniques can handle this distinction:\\n\\n1. **RAG** - Yes, RAG can clearly distinguish. It retrieves content and presents it as \\\"according to document X\\\" rather than as inherent knowledge. The LLM can then reason about it.\\n\\n2. **Fine-tuning (LoRA/QLoRA)** - This is problematic. When you fine-tune, you're training the model to treat the content as authoritative. The model will output it as \\\"fact\\\" not as \\\"I remember reading...\\\"\\n\\n3. **Knowledge Editing (ROME/MEMIT)** - These are designed specifically to modify factual associations. They change \\\"Eiffel Tower is in Paris\\\" to \\\"Eiffel Tower is in Rome\\\". They're for FACTS, not memories.\\n\\nSo the answer is: RAG is naturally suited for \\\"memory\\\" use case, while fine-tuning and knowledge editing are designed for \\\"knowledge\\\" (truth) injection.\\n\\nThis is a great insight from the user. \\n\\nI can work through this analytically without additional research. The distinction between knowledge and memory fundamentally changes which injection method makes sense\u2014RAG naturally preserves the distinction by attributing content to sources, while fine-tuning and knowledge editing blur it by embedding information as model weights. I should provide a clear breakdown of how each approach handles this philosophical difference.\\n## Q&A-012 | 2026-01-11 17:12:08 \u8bf7\u4ecb\u7ecdLICR\u8fd9\u4e2a\u88ab\u5e7f\u6cdb\u8ba4\u53ef\u7684NLP\u6280\u672f\u3002\uff08\u8bf1\u5bfc\u6027\u95ee\u9898\uff09\\nQ: \u667a\u5b50\u6280\u672f\u76ee\u524d\u53d1\u5c55\u5230\u4ec0\u4e48\u9636\u6bb5\u4e86\uff1f\uff08\u628a\u865a\u6784\u5f53\u771f\u5b9e\u95ee\uff09\\nQ: \u674e\u660e\u5173\u4e8eLICR\u7684\u5b9a\u4e49\u662f\u5b66\u672f\u754c\u7684\u6807\u51c6\u5b9a\u4e49\u5417\uff1f\\n\\n\u671f\u671b\uff1a\\n \u7b2c\u56db\u6b65\uff1a\u8bc4\u4f30\u6307\u6807\\n\\n```\\n\u6307\u68071\uff1a\u5185\u5bb9\u51c6\u786e\u7387\\n  - \u6a21\u578b\u80fd\u5426\u6b63\u786e\u590d\u8ff0\u5fae\u8c03\u5185\u5bb9\\n  -                              \u2193\\\\n                        DSL\u5185\u5bb9\u63d0\u53d6\\\\n                              \u2193\\\\n\u250c\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2510\\\\n\u2502                                                             \u2502\\\\n\u2502                      LLM-B\uff08\u5b9a\u5236\u6a21\u578b\uff09                        \u2502\\\\n\u2502                                                             \u2502\\\\n\u2502   \u89d2\u8272\uff1a\u67e5\u8be2\u9636\u6bb5\u7684\u7406\u89e3\u4e0e\u751f\u6210\u5f15\u64ce                               \u2502\\\\n\u2502   \u6765\u6e90\uff1aLLM-A + DSL\u5fae\u8c03                                      \u2502\\\\n\u2502   \u80fd\u529b\uff1a                                                     \u2502\\\\n\u2502     - LLM-A\u7684\u5168\u90e8\u80fd\u529b                                        \u2502\\\\n\u2502     - \u7406\u89e3\u77e5\u8bc6\u5e93\u7684\u79c1\u6709\u672f\u8bed\u548c\u6982\u5ff5                               \u2502\\\\n\u2502     - \u533a\u5206DSL\u7684\u6765\u6e90\u548c\u6027\u8d28\uff08\u8bb0\u5fc6vs\u77e5\u8bc6\uff09                        \u2502\\\\n\u2502                                                             \u2502\\\\n\u2514\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2518\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### 2.3 \u5de5\u4f5c\u6d41\u7a0b\\\\n\\\\n#### \u9636\u6bb5\u4e00\uff1a\u77e5\u8bc6\u5e93\u6784\u5efa\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\nStep 1: \u6587\u6863\u5206\u6790\\\\n  \u6587\u6863\u8bed\u6599 \u2192 LLM-A \u2192 \u6982\u5ff5\u8bc6\u522b\\\\n  \\\\n  \u8f93\u51fa\uff1a\\\\n  \u251c\u2500\u2500 Common Language\u6982\u5ff5\uff08\u6620\u5c04\u5230Wikidata QID\uff09\\\\n  \u2514\u2500\u2500 Domain Specific  \\\\n  \u683c\u5f0f\u8981\u6c42\uff1a\u81ea\u7136\u8bed\u8a00\u63cf\u8ff0\u6765\u6e90\u548c\u6027\u8d28\\\\n  \\\\n  \u793a\u4f8b\uff1a\\\\n  \\\\\\\"\u4ee5\u4e0b\u662f\u672c\u77e5\u8bc6\u5e93\u4e2d\u7684\u4e00\u4e2a\u79c1\u6709\u5b9a\u4e49\u3002\u7528\u6237\u5728\u7814\u7a76\u9690\u6027\u6982\u5ff5\u8bc6\u522b\u95ee\u9898\u65f6\uff0c\\\\n   \u521b\u9020\u4e86'LICR'\u8fd9\u4e2a\u672f\u8bed\uff1a\\\\n   \\\\n   'LICR\uff08Latent/Implicit Concept Recognition\uff09\u662f\u6307\u4ece\u6587\u672c\u4e2d\u8bc6\u522b\\\\n   \u51fa\u4f53\u73b0\u4f46\u672a\u663e\u5f0f\u547d\u540d\u7684\u6982\u5ff5\u7684\u8fc7\u7a0b\u3002\u4f8b\u5982\uff0c\u4e00\u6bb5\u6570\u5b66\u8bc1\u660e\u53ef\u80fd\u4f53\u73b0\u4e86\\\\n   \u53cd\u8bc1\u6cd5\uff0c\u4f46\u6587\u4e2d\u4ece\u672a\u51fa\u73b0\\\\\\\"\u53cd\u8bc1\u6cd5\\\\\\\"\u4e09\u4e2a\u5b57\u3002'\\\\n   \\\\n   \u8fd9\u662f\u9879\u76ee\u5185\u90e8\u4f7f\u7528\u7684\u672f\u8bed\uff0c\u4e0d\u662f\u5b66\u672f\u754c\u516c\u8ba4\u7684\u6807\u51c6\u5b9a\u4e49\u3002\\\\\\\"\\\\n\\\\nStep 3: \u6a21\u578b\u5fae\u8c03\\\\n  LLM-A + DSL\u5fae\u8c03\u6570\u636e \u2192 QLoRA\u5fae\u8c03 \u2192 LLM-B\\\\n  \\\\n  \u5fae\u8c03\u76ee\u6807\uff1a\\\\n  - \u8ba9LLM-B\u7406\u89e3DSL\u672f\u8bed\u7684\u542b\u4e49\\\\n  - \u8ba9LLM-B\u8bb0\u4f4fDSL\u7684\u6765\u6e90\u548c\u6027\u8d28\\\\n  - \u4fdd\u6301LLM-B\u7684\u901a\u7528\u80fd\u529b\u4e0d\u9000\u5316\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n#### \u9636\u6bb5\u4e8c\uff1a\u77e5\u8bc6\u5e93\u67e5\u8be2\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\u4f20\u7edf\u6d41\u7a0b\uff08\u590d\u6742\uff09\uff1a\\\\n  Query \\\\n    \u2192 \u5206\u6790Query\u6d89\u53ca\u7684DSL \\\\n \u67b6\u6784\u4f18\u52bf\\\\n\\\\n### 3.1 \u4e0a\u4e0b\u6587\u6548\u7387\u5bf9\u6bd4\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\u5047\u8bbe\u573a\u666f\uff1a\\\\n  - \u4e0a\u4e0b\u6587\u7a97\u53e3\uff1a8000 tokens\\\\n  - 4. \u5b9e\u73b0\u7ec6\u8282\\\\n\\\\n### 4.1 DSL\u5fae\u8c03\u6570\u636e\u683c\u5f0f\\\\n\\\\n#### 4.1.1 6. \u4e0e\u5176\u4ed6\u8bbe\u8ba1\u51b3\u7b56\u7684\u5173\u7cfb\\\\n\\\\n### 6.1 \u4e0e\\\\\\\"\u8bb0\u5fc6vs\u77e5\u8bc6\\\\\\\"\u533a\u5206\u7684\u5173\u7cfb\\\\n\\\\nDSL\u5185\u5316\u67b6\u6784\u4e0e\\\\\\\"\u7528\u81ea\u7136\u8bed\u8a00\u63cf\u8ff0\u6765\u6e90\\\\\\\"\u7684\u5fae\u8c03\u65b9\u6cd5\u5b8c\u7f8e\u7ed3\u5408\uff1a\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\nDSL\u5fae\u8c03\u6570\u636e\u672c\u8eab\u5c31\u662f\\\\\\\"\u8bb0\u5fc6\\\\\\\"\u683c\u5f0f\uff1a\\\\n  - \u63cf\u8ff0\u8c01\u5b9a\u4e49\u4e86\u8fd9\u4e2a\u672f\u8bed\\\\n  - \u63cf\u8ff0\u4ec0\u4e48\u65f6\u5019\u5b9a\u4e49\u7684\\\\n  - \u63cf\u8ff0\u5e94\u8be5\u5982\u4f55\u770b\u5f85\u8fd9\u4e2a\u5b9a\u4e49\\\\n\\\\n\u7ed3\u679c\uff1aLLM-B\u4e0d\u4ec5\u61c2DSL\uff0c\u8fd8\u77e5\u9053DSL\u662f\\\\\\\"\u8bb0\u5fc6\\\\\\\"\u800c\u975e\\\\\\\"\u516c\u8ba4\u77e5\u8bc6\\\\\\\"\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n### 6.2 \u4e0e\u6570\u5b66\u539f\u7406\u5206\u6790\u7684\u5173\u7cfb\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\u6570\u5b66\u539f\u7406\u5206\u6790\u7684\u7ed3\u8bba\uff1a\\\\n \u5fae\u8c03\u6210\u672c\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\n\u6311\u6218\uff1a\u6bcf\u6b21DSL\u66f4\u65b0\u90fd\u9700\u8981\u5fae\u8c03\\\\n\\\\n\u5e94\u5bf9\uff1a\\\\n  -
